Though it may seem\\r\\nsomewhat dull compared to some of the more modern statistical learning\\r\\napproaches described in later chapters of this book, linear regression is still\\r\\na useful and widely used statistical learning method. Moreover, it serves\\r\\nas a good jumping-oﬀpoint for newer approaches: as we will see in later\\r\\nchapters, many fancy statistical learning approaches can be seen as gener-\\r\\nalizations or extensions of linear regression. Consequently, the importance\\r\\nof having a good understanding of linear regression before studying more\\r\\ncomplex learning methods cannot be overstated. In this chapter, we review\\r\\nsome of the key ideas underlying the linear regression model, as well as the\\r\\nleast squares approach that is most commonly used to ﬁt this model.\\r\\nRecall the Advertising data from Chapter 2. What information would be useful in order to provide\\r\\nsuch a recommendation? Here are a few important questions that we might\\r\\nseek to address:\\r\\n1. Is there a relationship between advertising budget and sales?\\r\\nOur ﬁrst goal should be to determine whether the data provide\\r\\nG. It assumes that there is approximately a linear\\r\\nrelationship between X and Y . Mathematically, we can write this linear\\r\\nrelationship as\\r\\nY ≈β0 + β1X.\\r\\n(3.1)\\r\\nYou might read “≈” as “is approximately modeled as”. We will sometimes\\r\\ndescribe (3.1) by saying that we are regressing Y on X (or Y onto X).\\r\\nFor example, X may represent TV advertising and Y may represent sales.\\r\\nThen we can regress sales onto TV by ﬁtting the model\\r\\nsales ≈β0 + β1 × TV.\\r\\nIn Equation 3.1, β0 and β1 are two unknown constants that represent\\r\\nthe intercept and slope terms in the linear model. Together, β0 and β1 are intercept\\r\\nslope\\r\\nknown as the model coeﬃcients\\r\\nor parameters.\\r\\nOnce we have used our\\r\\ncoeﬃcient\\r\\nparameter\\r\\ntraining data to produce estimates ˆβ0 and ˆβ1 for the model coeﬃcients, we\\r\\ncan predict future sales on the basis of a particular value of TV advertising\\r\\nby computing\\r\\nˆy = ˆβ0 + ˆβ1x,\\r\\n(3.2)\\r\\nwhere ˆy indicates a prediction of Y on the basis of X = x. Here we use a\\r\\nhat symbol, ˆ , to denote the estimated value for an unknown parameter\\r\\nor coeﬃcient, or to denote the predicted value of the response.\\r\\n3.1.1\\r\\nEstimating the Coeﬃcients\\r\\nIn practice, β0 and β1 are unknown. So before we can use (3.1) to make\\r\\npredictions, we must use data to estimate the coeﬃcients. Let\\r\\n(x1, y1), (x2, y2), . . . , (xn, yn)\\r\\nrepresent n observation pairs, each of which consists of a measurement\\r\\nof X and a measurement of Y . In the Advertising example, this data\\r\\nset consists of the TV advertising budget and product sales in n = 200\\r\\ndiﬀerent markets. (Recall that the data are displayed in Figure 2.1.) Our\\r\\ngoal is to obtain coeﬃcient estimates ˆβ0 and ˆβ1 such that the linear model\\r\\n(3.1) ﬁts the available data well—that is, so that yi ≈ˆβ0 + ˆβ1xi for i =\\r\\n1, . . . , n. In other words, we want to ﬁnd an intercept ˆβ0 and a slope ˆβ1 such\\r\\nthat the resulting line is as close as possible to the n = 200 data points.\\r\\nThere are a number of ways of measuring closeness. However, by far the\\r\\nmost common approach involves minimizing the least squares criterion, least squares\\r\\nand we take that approach in this chapter. Alternative approaches will be\\r\\nconsidered in Chapter 6.\\r\\n\\r\\nML_260828_125033.pdf \\r\\n 3 | GEOMETRY AND NEAREST NEIGHBORS\\r\\nDependencies: Chapter 1\\r\\nYou can think of prediction tasks as mapping inputs (course\\r\\nreviews) to outputs (course ratings). In order\\r\\n\\r\\n7 | LINEAR MODELS\\r\\nDependencies:\\r\\nIn Chapter 4, you learned about the perceptron algorithm for\\r\\nlinear classiﬁcation. This was both a model (linear classiﬁer) and al-\\r\\ngorithm (the perceptron update rule) in one. In this section, we will\\r\\nseparate these two, and consider general ways for optimizing lin-\\r\\near models. This will lead us into some aspects of optimization (aka\\r\\nmathematical programming), but not very far. At the end of this\\r\\nchapter, there are pointers to more literature on optimization for\\r\\nthose who are interested.\\r\\nThe basic idea of the perceptron is to run a particular algorithm\\r\\nuntil a linear separator is found. You might ask: are there better al-\\r\\ngorithms for ﬁnding such a linear separator? We will follow this idea\\r\\nand formulate a learning problem as an explicit optimization prob-\\r\\nlem: ﬁnd me a linear separator that is not too complicated. We will\\r\\nsee that ﬁnding an “optimal” separator is actually computationally\\r\\nprohibitive, and so will need to “relax” the optimality requirement.\\r\\nThis will lead us to a convex objective that combines a loss func-\\r\\ntion (how well are we doing on the training data?) and a regularizer\\r\\n(how complicated is our learned model?). This learning framework\\r\\nis known as both Tikhonov regularization and structural risk mini-\\r\\nmization.\\r\\n7.1\\r\\nThe Optimization Framework for Linear Models\\r\\nYou have already seen the perceptron as a way of ﬁnding a weight\\r\\nvector w and bias b that do a good job of separating positive train-\\r\\ning examples from negative training examples. The perceptron is a\\r\\nmodel and algorithm in one. Here, we are interested in separating\\r\\nthese issues. We will focus on linear models, like the perceptron.\\r\\nBut we will think about other, more generic ways of ﬁnding good\\r\\nparameters of these models.\\r\\nThe goal of the perceptron was to ﬁnd a separating hyperplane\\r\\nfor some training data set. For simplicity, you can ignore the issue\\r\\nof overﬁtting (but just for now!). Not all data sets are linearly sepa-\\r\\nLearning Objectives:\\r\\n• Deﬁne and plot four surrogate loss\\r\\nfunctions: squared loss, logistic loss,\\r\\nexponential loss and hinge loss.\\r\\n• Compare and contrast the optimiza-\\r\\ntion of 0/1 loss and surrogate loss\\r\\nfunctions.\\r\\n• Solve the optimization problem\\r\\nfor squared loss with a quadratic\\r\\nregularizer in closed form.\\r\\n• Implement and debug gradient\\r\\ndescent and subgradient descent.\\r\\nThe essence of mathematics is not to make simple things compli-\\r\\ncated, but to make complicated things simple.\\r\\n– Stanley Gudder\\r\\n\\r\\n88\\r\\na course in machine learning\\r\\nrable. In the case that your training data isn’t linearly separable, you\\r\\nmight want to ﬁnd the hyperplane that makes the fewest errors on\\r\\nthe training data. We can write this down as a formal mathematics\\r\\noptimization problem as follows:\\r\\nmin\\r\\nw,b\\r\\n∑\\r\\nn\\r\\n1[yn(w · xn + b) > 0]\\r\\n(7.1)\\r\\nIn this expression, you are optimizing over two variables, w and b.\\r\\nThe objective function is the thing you are trying to minimize. In\\r\\nthis case, the objective function is simply the error rate (or 0/1 loss) of\\r\\nthe linear classiﬁer parameterized by w, b. In this expression, 1[·] is\\r\\nthe indicator function: it is one when (·) is true and zero otherwise.\\r\\nYou should remember the yw\\r\\n·\\r\\nx\\r\\ntrick from the perceptron discus-\\r\\nsion. If not, re-convince yourself\\r\\nthat this is doing the right thing.\\r\\n?\\r\\nWe know that the perceptron algorithm is guaranteed to ﬁnd\\r\\nparameters for this model if the data is linearly separable. In other\\r\\nwords, if the optimum of Eq (7.1) is zero, then the perceptron will\\r\\nefﬁciently ﬁnd parameters for this model. The notion of “efﬁciency”\\r\\ndepends on the margin of the data for the perceptron.\\r\\nYou might ask: what happens if the data is not linearly separable?\\r\\nIs there an efﬁcient algorithm for ﬁnding an optimal setting of the\\r\\nparameters? Unfortunately, the answer is no. There is no polynomial\\r\\ntime algorithm for solving Eq (7.1), unless P=NP. In other words,\\r\\nthis problem is NP-hard. Sadly, the proof of this is quite complicated\\r\\nand beyond the scope of this book, but it relies on a reduction from a\\r\\nvariant of satisﬁability. The key idea is to turn a satisﬁability problem\\r\\ninto an optimization problem where a clause is satisﬁed exactly when\\r\\nthe hyperplane correctly separates the data.\\r\\nYou might then come back and say: okay, well I don’t really need\\r\\nan exact solution. I’m willing to have a solution that makes one or\\r\\ntwo more errors than it has to. Unfortunately, the situation is really\\r\\nbad. Zero/one loss is NP-hard to even appproximately minimize. In\\r\\nother words, there is no efﬁcient algorithm for even ﬁnding a solution\\r\\nthat’s a small constant worse than optimal. (The best known constant\\r\\nat this time is 418/415 ≈1.007.)\\r\\nHowever, before getting too disillusioned about this whole enter-\\r\\nprise (remember: there’s an entire chapter about this framework, so\\r\\nit must be going somewhere!), you should remember that optimizing\\r\\nEq (7.1) perhaps isn’t even what you want to do! In particular, all it\\r\\nsays is that you will get minimal training error. It says nothing about\\r\\nwhat your test error will be like. In order to try to ﬁnd a solution that\\r\\nwill generalize well to test data, you need to ensure that you do not\\r\\noverﬁt the data. To do this, you can introduce a regularizer over the\\r\\nparameters of the model. For now, we will be vague about what this\\r\\nregularizer looks like, and simply call it an arbitrary function R(w, b).\\r\\n\\r\\nlinear models\\r\\n89\\r\\nThis leads to the following, regularized objective:\\r\\nmin\\r\\nw,b\\r\\n∑\\r\\nn\\r\\n1[yn(w · xn + b) > 0] + λR(w, b)\\r\\n(7.2)\\r\\nIn Eq (7.2), we are now trying to optimize a trade-off between a so-\\r\\nlution that gives low training error (the ﬁrst term) and a solution\\r\\nthat is “simple” (the second term). You can think of the maximum\\r\\ndepth hyperparameter of a decision tree as a form of regularization\\r\\nfor trees. Here, R is a form of regularization for hyperplanes. In this\\r\\nformulation, λ becomes a hyperparameter for the optimization.\\r\\nAssuming R does the “right thing,”\\r\\nwhat value(s) of λ will lead to over-\\r\\nﬁtting? What value(s) will lead to\\r\\nunderﬁtting?\\r\\n?\\r\\nThe key remaining questions, given this formalism, are:\\r\\n• How can we adjust the optimization problem so that there are\\r\\nefﬁcient algorithms for solving it?\\r\\n• What are good regularizers R(w, b) for hyperplanes?\\r\\n• Assuming we can adjust the optimization problem appropriately,\\r\\nwhat algorithms exist for efﬁciently solving this regularized opti-\\r\\nmization problem?\\r\\nWe will address these three questions in the next sections.\\r\\n7.2\\r\\nConvex Surrogate Loss Functions\\r\\nYou might ask: why is optimizing zero/one loss so hard? Intuitively,\\r\\none reason is that small changes to w, b can have a large impact on\\r\\nthe value of the objective function. For instance, if there is a positive\\r\\ntraining example with w, x · +b = −0.0000001, then adjusting b up-\\r\\nwards by 0.00000011 will decrease your error rate by 1. But adjusting\\r\\nit upwards by 0.00000009 will have no effect. This makes it really\\r\\ndifﬁcult to ﬁgure out good ways to adjust the parameters.\\r\\nFigure 7.1: plot of zero/one versus\\r\\nmargin\\r\\nTo see this more clearly, it is useful to look at plots that relate\\r\\nmargin to loss. Such a plot for zero/one loss is shown in Figure 7.1.\\r\\nIn this plot, the horizontal axis measures the margin of a data point\\r\\nand the vertical axis measures the loss associated with that margin.\\r\\nFor zero/one loss, the story is simple. If you get a positive margin\\r\\n(i.e., y(w · x + b) > 0) then you get a loss of zero. Otherwise you get\\r\\na loss of one. By thinking about this plot, you can see how changes\\r\\nto the parameters that change the margin just a little bit can have an\\r\\nenormous effect on the overall loss.\\r\\nFigure 7.2: plot of zero/one versus\\r\\nmargin and an S version of it\\r\\nYou might decide that a reasonable way to address this problem is\\r\\nto replace the non-smooth zero/one loss with a smooth approxima-\\r\\ntion. With a bit of effort, you could probably concoct an “S”-shaped\\r\\nfunction like that shown in Figure 7.2. The beneﬁt of using such an\\r\\nS-function is that it is smooth, and potentially easier to optimize. The\\r\\ndifﬁculty is that it is not convex.\\r\\n\\r\\n90\\r\\na course in machine learning\\r\\nIf you remember from calculus, a convex function is one that looks\\r\\nlike a happy face (⌣). (On the other hand, a concave function is one\\r\\nthat looks like a sad face (⌢); an easy mnemonic is that you can hide\\r\\nunder a concave function.) There are two equivalent deﬁnitions of\\r\\na convex function. The ﬁrst is that it’s second derivative is always\\r\\nnon-negative. The second, more geometric, deﬁtion is that any chord\\r\\nof the function lies above it. This is shown in Figure 7.3. There you\\r\\ncan see a convex function and a non-convex function, both with two\\r\\nchords drawn in. In the case of the convex function, the chords lie\\r\\nabove the function. In the case of the non-convex function, there are\\r\\nparts of the chord that lie below the function.\\r\\nFigure 7.3: plot of convex and non-\\r\\nconvex functions with two chords each\\r\\nConvex functions are nice because they are easy to minimize. Intu-\\r\\nitively, if you drop a ball anywhere in a convex function, it will even-\\r\\ntually get to the minimum. This is not true for non-convex functions.\\r\\nFor example, if you drop a ball on the very left end of the S-function\\r\\nfrom Figure 7.2, it will not go anywhere.\\r\\nThis leads to the idea of convex surrogate loss functions. Since\\r\\nzero/one loss is hard to optimize, you want to optimize something\\r\\nelse, instead. Since convex functions are easy to optimize, we want\\r\\nto approximate zero/one loss with a convex function. This approxi-\\r\\nmating function will be called a surrogate loss. The surrogate losses\\r\\nwe construct will always be upper bounds on the true loss function:\\r\\nthis guarantees that if you minimize the surrogate loss, you are also\\r\\npushing down the real loss.\\r\\nFigure 7.4: surrogate loss fns\\r\\nThere are four common surrogate loss functions, each with their\\r\\nown properties: hinge loss, logistic loss, exponential loss and\\r\\nsquared loss. These are shown in Figure 7.4 and deﬁned below.\\r\\nThese are deﬁned in terms of the true label y (which is just {−1, +1})\\r\\nand the predicted value ˆy = w · x + b.\\r\\nZero/one:\\r\\nℓ(0/1)(y, ˆy) = 1[y ˆy ≤0]\\r\\n(7.3)\\r\\nHinge:\\r\\nℓ(hin)(y, ˆy) = max{0, 1 −y ˆy}\\r\\n(7.4)\\r\\nLogistic:\\r\\nℓ(log)(y, ˆy) =\\r\\n1\\r\\nlog 2 log (1 + exp[−y ˆy])\\r\\n(7.5)\\r\\nExponential:\\r\\nℓ(exp)(y, ˆy) = exp[−y ˆy]\\r\\n(7.6)\\r\\nSquared:\\r\\nℓ(sqr)(y, ˆy) = (y −ˆy)2\\r\\n(7.7)\\r\\nIn the deﬁnition of logistic loss, the\\r\\n1\\r\\nlog 2 term out front is there sim-\\r\\nply to ensure that ℓ(log)(y, 0) = 1. This ensures, like all the other\\r\\nsurrogate loss functions, that logistic loss upper bounds the zero/one\\r\\nloss. (In practice, people typically omit this constant since it does not\\r\\naffect the optimization.)\\r\\nThere are two big differences in these loss functions. The ﬁrst\\r\\ndifference is how “upset” they get by erroneous predictions. In the\\r\\n\\r\\n\"}"
  },
  {
    "type": "input_text",
    "text": "{}"
  }
]

Los puntos grandes son los vecinos seleccionados.'} Empates de voto: clase menor; de distancia: orden de los puntos.</p>`+table(['Vecino','Distancia','Target'],ns.map(n=>['('+n.x.join(', ')+')',fmt(n.d),n.y]));};\n+ }else if(id==='distances'){\n+  controls.innerHTML=select('p','Norma',[['1','L₁ · rombo'],['2','L₂ · círculo'],['4','L₄'],['Infinity','L∞ · cuadrado']])+slider('dx','Diferencia |Δx₁|',0,5,.1,3)+slider('dy','Diferencia |Δx₂|',0,5,.1,4)+slider('sigma','σ · similitud gaussiana',.1,5,.1,2);\n+  draw=()=>{const p=value('p'),x=value('dx'),y=value('dy'),sigma=value('sigma');let pts=[];for(let t=0;t<=Math.PI*2+.01;t+=.025){let a=Math.cos(t),b=Math.sin(t),norm=p===Infinity?Math.max(Math.abs(a),Math.abs(b)):(Math.abs(a)**p+Math.abs(b)**p)**(1/p);pts.push(`${300+120*a/norm},${170-120*b/norm}`);}out.innerHTML=svg(`<path d=\"M100 170H500 M300 15V325\" stroke=\"var(--muted)\"/><polyline points=\"${pts.join(' ')}\" fill=\"#bce3df88\" stroke=\"#127e80\" stroke-width=\"3\"/><text x=\"430\" y=\"192\">1</text><text x=\"310\" y=\"45\">1</text><text x=\"300\" y=\"352\" text-anchor=\"middle\">Frontera a distancia 1 del origen · L${p===Infinity?'∞':p}</text>`,'Bola unitaria de la norma seleccionada')+`<div class=\"lab-stats\">${stat('L₁',fmt(x+y))}${stat('L₂',fmt(Math.hypot(x,y)))}${stat('L∞',fmt(Math.max(x,y)))}</div><p>Para Δ=(${x}, ${y}), L₂ = √(${fmt(x*x)} + ${fmt(y*y)}).</p><p>Similitud gaussiana: exp(−${fmt(x*x+y*y)}/(2·${sigma}²)) = <strong>${fmt(Math.exp(-(x*x+y*y)/(2*sigma*sigma)))}</strong>.</p><p class=\"caption\">La curva representa la bola unitaria; los controles Δ calculan un ejemplo separado. La similitud usa L₂ independientemente de la norma dibujada.</p>`;};\n+ }else if(id==='regression'){\n+  controls.innerHTML=slider('a','Pendiente · a',-.5,2,.05,.6)+slider('b','Intercepto · b',-2,4,.1,1)+select('outlier','Observación atípica',[['0','Sin outlier'],['1','Cambiar último target a 2']]);\n+  draw=()=>{const a=value('a'),b=value('b');const pts=demoPoints.map((v,i)=>({...v,y:value('outlier')&&i===7?2:v.y}));out.innerHTML=plot(`<defs><clipPath id=\"graph-clip\"><rect x=\"45\" y=\"20\" width=\"525\" height=\"290\"/></clipPath></defs><g clip-path=\"url(#graph-clip)\">${pts.map(v=>`<path d=\"M${sx(v.x)} ${sy(v.y)}V${sy(a*v.x+b)}\" stroke=\"#df7859\" stroke-dasharray=\"4 3\"/>`).join('')}<path d=\"M${sx(0)} ${sy(b)}L${sx(10)} ${sy(10*a+b)}\" stroke=\"#127e80\" stroke-width=\"3\"/>${pts.map(v=>dot(v.x,v.y)).join('')}</g>`,'Recta y residuos verticales')+`<div class=\"lab-stats\">${stat('MSE',fmt(mse(pts,a,b)))}${stat('R²',fmt(r2(pts,a,b)))}${stat('Modelo',a+'x + '+b)}</div>`+table(['x','y real','ŷ','(ŷ − y)²'],pts.map(v=>[v.x,v.y,fmt(a*v.x+b),fmt((a*v.x+b-v.y)**2)]))+`<p class=\"caption\">Ajusta la recta para reducir MSE y después activa el outlier. Las cifras evalúan estos mismos ocho puntos, no una prueba de generalización.</p>`;};\n+ }else if(id==='gradient'){\n+  let a=0,b=0,iteration=0,history=[mse(demoPoints,0,0)],message='';\n+  controls.innerHTML=slider('rate','Learning rate · η',.001,.1,.001,.01)+`<div class=\"button-row\"><button class=\"primary\" id=\"one\">Dar un paso</button><button class=\"secondary\" id=\"ten\">10 pasos</button><button class=\"secondary\" id=\"reset\">Reiniciar</button></div><p class=\"caption\">Full batch sobre 8 puntos. El gradiente mantiene el factor 2/n. Una tasa alta puede divergir.</p>`;\n+  draw=()=>{const g=gradient(demoPoints,a,b);const max=Math.max(...history,1);out.innerHTML=svg(`<rect x=\"45\" y=\"20\" width=\"525\" height=\"290\" rx=\"8\" fill=\"var(--plot)\"/><path d=\"M45 20V310H570\" stroke=\"var(--muted)\" fill=\"none\"/><polyline fill=\"none\" stroke=\"#127e80\" stroke-width=\"3\" points=\"${history.map((v,i)=>`${45+i/Math.max(history.length-1,1)*525},${310-v/max*270}`).join(' ')}\"/><text x=\"50\" y=\"16\">MSE · máximo ${fmt(max)}</text><text x=\"300\" y=\"346\" text-anchor=\"middle\">Actualizaciones: 0 → ${iteration}</text>`,'Evolución de MSE con cada paso de gradiente')+`<div class=\"lab-stats\">${stat('Paso',iteration)}${stat('MSE',fmt(history.at(-1)))}${stat('a / b',fmt(a)+' / '+fmt(b))}</div><div class=\"calculation\">∂L/∂a = ${fmt(g[0])}<br>∂L/∂b = ${fmt(g[1])}<br>a siguiente = ${fmt(a)} − ${value('rate')} · (${fmt(g[0])})<br>b siguiente = ${fmt(b)} − ${value('rate')} · (${fmt(g[1])})</div><p role=\"status\">${message||'Los dos gradientes se calculan antes de modificar a y b.'}</p>`;};\n+  const advance=n=>{for(let i=0;i<n;i++){const [na,nb]=step(demoPoints,a,b,value('rate'));const loss=mse(demoPoints,na,nb);if(!Number.isFinite(loss)||loss>1e12){message='Divergencia: se detuvo el cálculo antes de exceder el límite numérico. Reduce η y reinicia.';break;}a=na;b=nb;iteration++;history.push(loss);message=loss>history.at(-2)?'La pérdida aumentó. Observa la tasa de aprendizaje.':'';}draw();};controls.querySelector('#one').onclick=()=>advance(1);controls.querySelector('#ten').onclick=()=>advance(10);controls.querySelector('#reset').onclick=()=>{a=b=iteration=0;history=[mse(demoPoints,0,0)];message='';draw();};\n+ }else if(id==='matrix'){\n+  controls.innerHTML=slider('w1','Peso w₁',-4,4,1,2)+slider('w2','Peso w₂',-4,4,1,-1)+slider('mb','Intercepto b',-3,3,1,1);\n+  draw=()=>{const w1=value('w1'),w2=value('w2'),b=value('mb');out.innerHTML=`<div class=\"matrix-diagram\"><div><small>X · 2 × 2</small><pre>┌ 1  2 ┐\n+└ 3  4 ┘</pre></div><strong>×</strong><div><small>w · 2 × 1</small><pre>┌ ${w1} ┐\n+└ ${w2} ┘</pre></div><strong>+</strong><div><small>b · 1</small><pre>┌ ${b} ┐\n+└ ${b} ┘</pre></div></div><h3>Fila por columna</h3><div class=\"calculation\">ŷ₁ = 1 × (${w1}) + 2 × (${w2}) + (${b}) = <b>${w1+2*w2+b}</b><br>ŷ₂ = 3 × (${w1}) + 4 × (${w2}) + (${b}) = <b>${3*w1+4*w2+b}</b></div><p>Las dimensiones interiores coinciden: (2 × <strong>2</strong>)(<strong>2</strong> × 1) → 2 × 1.</p><p class=\"caption\">El resultado tiene una predicción por fila. Este es el mismo cálculo que usa un modelo de regresión con dos características.</p>`;};\n+ }else if(id==='scaling'){\n+  controls.innerHTML=slider('newx','Nueva observación x',0,12,.5,8);\n+  draw=()=>{const mean=4,sigma=Math.sqrt(8/3),x=value('newx');out.innerHTML=`<h3>Aprender la escala de train</h3>${table(['Etapa','Cálculo','Resultado'],[['Media μ','(2 + 4 + 6) / 3',4],['Varianza','(4 + 0 + 4) / 3',fmt(8/3)],['Desviación σ','√(8/3)',fmt(sigma)]])}<div class=\"lab-stats\">${[2,4,6].map(v=>stat('Train x = '+v,fmt((v-mean)/sigma))).join('')}</div><div class=\"calculation\">z nuevo = (${x} − 4) / ${fmt(sigma)} = <strong>${fmt((x-mean)/sigma)}</strong></div><p>El dato nuevo cambia; μ y σ se mantienen. Reajustar el escalador con la nueva observación alteraría las coordenadas aprendidas.</p>`;};\n+ }else{\n+  controls.innerHTML=slider('dim','Dimensión · d',2,1000,1,10)+slider('ratio','Radio interior / exterior',.5,.99,.01,.9);\n+  draw=()=>{const d=value('dim'),ratio=value('ratio'),inside=ratio**d;out.innerHTML=svg(`<circle cx=\"300\" cy=\"165\" r=\"130\" fill=\"#e9d9ee\" stroke=\"#a877b9\" stroke-width=\"2\"/><circle cx=\"300\" cy=\"165\" r=\"${130*ratio}\" fill=\"#c2e3df\" stroke=\"#127e80\" stroke-width=\"2\"/><path d=\"M300 165H430\" stroke=\"#324d52\"/><text x=\"305\" y=\"155\">R = 1</text><text x=\"300\" y=\"325\" text-anchor=\"middle\">Esquema en 2D · las proporciones se calculan en d=${d}</text>`,'Esquema de radios interior y exterior')+`<div class=\"lab-stats\">${stat('Fracción interior',fmt(inside))}${stat('Cáscara',fmt((1-inside)*100)+'%')}</div><div class=\"calculation\">(${ratio})<sup>${d}</sup> = ${inside.toExponential(5)}</div><p>Para un millón de puntos uniformes, el número esperado en el interior es <strong>${fmt(1e6*inside)}</strong>.</p><p class=\"caption\">El dibujo no representa literalmente un objeto de ${d} dimensiones. La hipótesis uniforme no describe necesariamente datos reales.</p>`;};\n+ }\n+ controls.querySelectorAll('input,select').forEach(input=>input.addEventListener('input',()=>{const label=controls.querySelector('#'+input.id+'-value');if(label)label.textContent=input.value;draw();}));draw();\n+}\n*** End Patch"));
```

Observa la tasa de aprendizaje.':'';}draw();};controls.querySelector('#one').onclick=()=>advance(1);controls.querySelector('#ten').onclick=()=>advance(10);controls.querySelector('#reset').onclick=()=>{a=b=iteration=0;history=[mse(demoPoints,0,0)];message='';draw();};\n }else if(id==='matrix'){\n  controls.innerHTML=slider('w1','Peso w₁',-4,4,1,2)+slider('w2','Peso w₂',-4,4,1,-1)+slider('mb','Intercepto b',-3,3,1,1);\n  draw=()=>{const w1=value('w1'),w2=value('w2'),b=value('mb');out.innerHTML=`<div class=\"matrix-diagram\"><div><small>X · 2 × 2</small><pre>┌ 1  2 ┐\n└ 3  4 ┘</pre></div><strong>×</strong><div><small>w · 2 × 1</small><pre>┌ ${w1} ┐\n└ ${w2} ┘</pre></div><strong>+</strong><div><small>b · 1</small><pre>┌ ${b} ┐\n└ ${b} ┘</pre></div></div><h3>Fila por columna</h3><div class=\"calculation\">ŷ₁ = 1 × (${w1}) + 2 × (${w2}) + (${b}) = <b>${w1+2*w2+b}</b><br>ŷ₂ = 3 × (${w1}) + 4 × (${w2}) + (${b}) = <b>${3*w1+4*w2+b}</b></div><p>Las dimensiones interiores coinciden: (2 × <strong>2</strong>)(<strong>2</strong> × 1) → 2 × 1.</p><p class=\"caption\">El resultado tiene una predicción por fila. Este es el mismo cálculo que usa un modelo de regresión con dos características.</p>`;};\n }else if(id==='scaling'){\n  controls.innerHTML=slider('newx','Nueva observación x',0,12,.5,8);\n  draw=()=>{const mean=4,sigma=Math.sqrt(8/3),x=value('newx');out.innerHTML=`<h3>Aprender la escala de train</h3>${table(['Etapa','Cálculo','Resultado'],[['Media μ','(2 + 4 + 6) / 3',4],['Varianza','(4 + 0 + 4) / 3',fmt(8/3)],['Desviación σ','√(8/3)',fmt(sigma)]])}<div class=\"lab-stats\">${[2,4,6].map(v=>stat('Train x = '+v,fmt((v-mean)/sigma))).join('')}</div><div class=\"calculation\">z nuevo = (${x} − 4) / ${fmt(sigma)} = <strong>${fmt((x-mean)/sigma)}</strong></div><p>El dato nuevo cambia; μ y σ se mantienen. Reajustar el escalador con la nueva observación alteraría las coordenadas aprendidas.</p>`;};\n }else{\n  controls.innerHTML=slider('dim','Dimensión · d',2,1000,1,10)+slider('ratio','Radio interior / exterior',.5,.99,.01,.9);\n  draw=()=>{const d=value('dim'),ratio=value('ratio'),inside=ratio**d;out.innerHTML=svg(`<circle cx=\"300\" cy=\"165\" r=\"130\" fill=\"#e9d9ee\" stroke=\"#a877b9\" stroke-width=\"2\"/><circle cx=\"300\" cy=\"165\" r=\"${130*ratio}\" fill=\"#c2e3df\" stroke=\"#127e80\" stroke-width=\"2\"/><path d=\"M300 165H430\" stroke=\"#324d52\"/><text x=\"305\" y=\"155\">R = 1</text><text x=\"300\" y=\"325\" text-anchor=\"middle\">Esquema en 2D · las proporciones se calculan en d=${d}</text>`,'Esquema de radios interior y exterior')+`<div class=\"lab-stats\">${stat('Fracción interior',fmt(inside))}${stat('Cáscara',fmt((1-inside)*100)+'%')}</div><div class=\"calculation\">(${ratio})<sup>${d}</sup> = ${inside.toExponential(5)}</div><p>Para un millón de puntos uniformes, el número esperado en el interior es <strong>${fmt(1e6*inside)}</strong>.</p><p class=\"caption\">El dibujo no representa literalmente un objeto de ${d} dimensiones. La hipótesis uniforme no describe necesariamente datos reales.</p>`;};\n }\n controls.querySelectorAll('input,select').forEach(input=>input.addEventListener('input',()=>{const label=controls.querySelector('#'+input.id+'-value');if(label)label.textContent=input.value;draw();}));draw();\n}\n"
    }

El syllabus también requiere otras nociones durante el semestre, pero no se marcaron como ya cursadas. Python se aprende practicando en notebooks; los quizzes conceptuales de esta página no exigen sintaxis Python.\n+\n+## Precisiones matemáticas y de código\n+\n+Estas son aclaraciones añadidas por el portal, no modificaciones de los archivos del profesor:\n+\n+1. **M1 §1.4:** una matriz de vectores de características aparece rotulada `y`; se presenta correctamente como `X` y las etiquetas como `y`.\n+2. **M2 §2.2:** la norma de Minkowski general necesita subíndice `p`; para ser una métrica se requiere `p≥1`. Para `0<p<1` la expresión no cumple desigualdad triangular.\n+3. **M2 §2.8:** calcular todas las distancias cuesta O(nd), y ordenar todas añade O(n log n). La búsqueda eficiente por índices depende de geometría y dimensión; no se promete O(d log n) universal.\n+4. **Assignment 1, celda 85:** `c >= k//2` no implementa mayoría estricta. Para k=5, dos votos positivos ya ganan; para k=1, incluso cero votos positivos ganan. El portal enseña mayoría correcta y una política explícita de empates. La condición también está en la copia personal y se conserva intacta.\n+5. **Assignment 1, texto sobre reshape:** reshape conserva el número de elementos. La repetición/truncamiento corresponde a resize; no son intercambiables.\n+6. **M3 §3.2.1:** MSE de regresión lineal es convexa, pero unicidad de los coeficientes requiere rango completo de la matriz de diseño. El mínimo no tiene por qué ser cero.\n+7. **M3 §3.3.1:** para f(t)=t², t siguiente=(1−2η)t: converge con 0<η<1, oscila con η=1 desde un punto no nulo y diverge con η>1. No extrapolar ese límite a pérdidas diferentes.\n+8. **M3 §3.3.2:** f(a,b)=a²+3ab+b²+3 sirve para derivar parcialmente, pero no debe ilustrar una función con mínimo: su Hessiano tiene eigenvalores 5 y −1. Las demos usan MSE convexa.\n+9. **M3 §3.5:** el módulo absorbe 2/n en la tasa ρ. El portal mantiene el factor explícito y explica la diferencia para que tasas numéricas no se comparen directamente.\n+10. **Tutoría 2 A:** los pesos e intercepto son parámetros; η, epochs y tamaño de lote son hiperparámetros. Para condiciones estacionarias se iguala a cero la primera derivada; PCA no equivale al ajuste OLS por ecuaciones normales.\n+11. **M3 §3.8:** R² puede ser negativo. El denominador de R² estándar usa la media del conjunto evaluado; una baseline desplegable se entrena con la media de train. Target constante necesita tratamiento especial.\n+12. **M3 §3.9:** se explica el bucle de propuestas de RANSAC además del pseudocódigo abreviado del módulo.\n+13. **M3 §3.10:** una banda basada en desviación incluye raíz; no es literalmente una característica polinómica cuadrática. No se convierte ese ejemplo en consejo financiero.\n+14. **Tutoría 2 B:** las fronteras entre rombo y círculo corresponden a 1<p<2. El experimento de la cáscara usa valores recalculados y declara la hipótesis uniforme; no afirma que toda distancia sea inútil en dimensión alta.\n+\n+## Lecturas complementarias\n+\n+Se extrajo texto de los tres PDFs para identificarlos y ubicar el material pertinente.
